It doesn’t stay in one lane. Like, at first I thought it was just about the condo stuff—because there’s always something with the condo, right? Maintenance, neighbors, fees, whatever—but then it’s like my brain just… jumps.\n\nI’ll start worrying that my cousin is upset with me because she texted a little differently, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I’m going to mess up on a support call at work, and then it turns into this whole thing about money—like how am I ever going to save enough or move forward in my life. It’s not really one topic. It sort of bounces around.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after something particularly stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it really started around when the condo loan started heading toward foreclosure. That was, um, about nine months ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because of that specific mess—like all the paperwork and calls, and I kept thinking about the mail piling up even when it was just normal junk mail—but then it kind of stayed with me. So… yes, there was definitely that stressful thing, and the feelings have been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get upset and kind of worn out, but “depressed” feels like a big word? I guess I have days where I’m just not in a great mood, especially if something annoying happens—like yesterday my printer decided to stop connecting to Wi-Fi and I spent way too long messing with it, which is ridiculous because I troubleshoot stuff for work.\n\nBut, yeah… I’m not sure I’d say I feel down a lot of the time. I haven’t really been tracking it like that.\n\nDoctor: For the past six months or more, have you felt worried or on edge most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think so. It’s been about nine months now, since the whole condo loan thing started moving toward foreclosure. At first I kept telling myself, like, anyone would be stressed about that, which is probably true, but it didn’t really let up. It’s just sort of been there in the background most days since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Most nights I’m exhausted by the time I get into bed, but then my brain just… turns on. I’ll start thinking about the loan notices, or some work ticket I maybe didn’t handle right, or something I forgot to check—like it becomes this whole mental checklist.\n\nAnd then suddenly it’s 2 or 3 in the morning, and I’m still awake. Even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I barely rested. It’s frustrating, because I’ll be so tired, but I just can’t shut it off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It just keeps looping back.\n\nAnd then one little “what if” turns into, like, five more—like what if I missed something, what if someone notices, what if it causes a bigger problem… I mean, it sounds kind of silly when I say it out loud, because it’s usually not that dramatic. But in the moment, it takes a while before I can pull myself back from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different areas of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It doesn’t stay in one lane. Like, at first I thought it was just about the condo stuff—because there’s always something with the condo, right? Maintenance, neighbors, fees, whatever—but then it’s like my brain just… jumps.\n\nI’ll start worrying that my cousin is upset with me because she texted a little differently, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I’m going to mess up on a support call at work, and then it turns into this whole thing about money—like how am I ever going to save enough or move forward in my life. It’s not really one topic. It sort of bounces around.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after something particularly stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it really started around when the condo loan started heading toward foreclosure. But in the moment, it takes a while before I can pull myself back from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different areas of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It doesn’t stay in one lane. Like, at first I thought it was just about the condo stuff—because there’s always something with the condo, right? Maintenance, neighbors, fees, whatever—but then it’s like my brain just… jumps.\n\nI’ll start worrying that my cousin is upset with me because she texted a little differently, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I’m going to mess up on a support call at work, and then it turns into this whole thing about money—like how am I ever going to save enough or move forward in my life. It’s not really one topic. It sort of bounces around.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after something particularly stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it really started around when the condo loan started heading toward foreclosure. That was, um, about nine months ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because of that specific mess—like all the paperwork and calls, and I kept thinking about the mail piling up even when it was just normal junk mail—but then it kind of stayed with me. So… yes, there was definitely that stressful thing, and the feelings have been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get upset and kind of worn out, but “depressed” feels like a big word? I guess I have days where I’m just not in a great mood, especially if something annoying happens—like yesterday my printer decided to stop connecting to Wi-Fi and I spent way too long messing with it, which is ridiculous because I troubleshoot stuff for work.\n\nBut, yeah… I’m not sure I’d say I feel down a lot of the time. I haven’t really been tracking it like that.\n\nDoctor: For the past six months or more, have you felt worried or on edge most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think so. It’s been about nine months now, since the whole condo loan thing started moving toward foreclosure. At first I kept telling myself, like, anyone would be stressed about that, which is probably true, but it didn’t really let up. It’s just sort of been there in the background most days since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Most nights I’m exhausted by the time I get into bed, but then my brain just… turns on. I’ll start thinking about the loan notices, or some work ticket I maybe didn’t handle right, or something I forgot to check—like it becomes this whole mental checklist.\n\nAnd then suddenly it’s 2 or 3 in the morning, and I’m still awake. Even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I barely rested. It’s frustrating, because I’ll be so tired, but I just can’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry is stronger than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the condo thing is a real problem, so part of me keeps saying, of course I’m worried. But it doesn’t stay there. It jumps around to everything—like whether my cousin is mad at me because she replied with just “okay,” or whether I’m going to mess up a support call at work, or how I’m ever supposed to save money and actually move forward.\n\nAnd then I’ll be doing something totally normal, like making coffee, and suddenly I’m thinking through five different things that could go wrong. So… yeah, I guess it probably gets bigger than the situation sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I feel kind of wound up almost all the time, like my body is bracing for the next bad thing even when I’m just sitting at my desk. My shoulders especially get tight, and sometimes I don’t even notice until I’m like, why am I sitting like this?\n\nIt’s weird because even at dance night, which is supposed to be fun, I’ll catch myself kind of holding tension instead of just relaxing into it. Then I start thinking about whether I look awkward, which—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, I notice the tightness a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like most of your distress is mainly tied to the condo foreclosure situation?\n\nPatient: I mean, it started with the condo, and that’s still the big thing, obviously. But no… it doesn’t really stay there anymore.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to focus on something else and suddenly I’m worried my cousin is upset with me because of how she texted back, or I’m replaying a support call in my head and thinking I’m going to make some mistake, or I’m spiraling about how I’m ever supposed to save money and move forward. It kind of spreads into everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is busy with worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. Like at work I’ll be writing a script or helping someone on a support call—in English or Spanish—and then suddenly I’m just… staring at the screen. I’ll realize I’ve drifted into thinking about everything that could go wrong, and I completely lose the thread of what I was doing.\n\nSometimes I have to reread the same thing a few times, which is frustrating because normally I’m pretty good at that stuff. And then I start worrying that taking too long looks bad, which kind of makes it worse.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s been about nine months now, since the whole condo loan thing started moving toward foreclosure. At first I kept telling myself, like, anyone would be stressed about that, which is probably true, but it didn’t really let up. It’s just sort of been there in the background most days since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Most nights I’m exhausted by the time I get into bed, but then my brain just… turns on. Even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I barely rested. It’s frustrating, because I’ll be so tired, but I just can’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry is stronger than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the condo thing is a real problem, so part of me keeps saying, of course I’m worried. But it doesn’t stay there. It jumps around to everything—like whether my cousin is mad at me because she replied with just “okay,” or whether I’m going to mess up a support call at work, or how I’m ever supposed to save money and actually move forward.\n\nAnd then I’ll be doing something totally normal, like making coffee, and suddenly I’m thinking through five different things that could go wrong. It kind of spreads into everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is busy with worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. I feel kind of wound up almost all the time, like my body is bracing for the next bad thing even when I’m just sitting at my desk. My shoulders especially get tight, and sometimes I don’t even notice until I’m like, why am I sitting like this?\n\nIt’s weird because even at dance night, which is supposed to be fun, I’ll catch myself kind of holding tension instead of just relaxing into it. Then I start thinking about whether I look awkward, which—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, I notice the tightness a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like most of your distress is mainly tied to the condo foreclosure situation?\n\nPatient: I mean, it started with the condo, and that’s still the big thing, obviously. But no… it doesn’t really stay there anymore.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to focus on something else and suddenly I’m worried my cousin is upset with me because of how she texted back, or I’m replaying a support call in my head and thinking I’m going to make some mistake, or I’m spiraling about how I’m ever supposed to save money and move forward. It kind of spreads into everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is busy with worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. Like at work I’ll be writing a script or helping someone on a support call—in English or Spanish—and then suddenly I’m just… staring at the screen. I’ll realize I’ve drifted into thinking about everything that could go wrong, and I completely lose the thread of what I was doing.\n\nSometimes I have to reread the same thing a few times, which is frustrating because normally I’m pretty good at that stuff. And then I start worrying that taking too long looks bad, which kind of makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: If the condo foreclosure were fully resolved tomorrow, do you think the anxiety would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I don’t think so. I wish I could say yes, because the condo thing is huge, but the worry doesn’t really stay there anymore.\n\nEven if that disappeared tomorrow, I think my brain would just grab onto something else—like whether my cousin is mad at me because her text sounded short, or whether I’m going to mess up on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and actually move forward. It’s like it jumps topics.\n\nI mean, resolving the condo would help, definitely. But I don’t think it would make the anxiety mostly go away.\n\nDoctor: Has this anxiety been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I keep wanting to say I’m managing, because technically I still show up to work, but I’ve started falling behind on support tickets. And sometimes I’ll double-check really simple things over and over, like something I know how to do, and then I lose time.\n\nA couple coworkers have had to pick up the slack, which I feel awful about. And outside of work, I’ve skipped dance nights and mentoring sessions because I just feel too overwhelmed to go. Which is frustrating, because those are things I actually care about, and normally they help me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Before the condo foreclosure started, were you already someone who felt anxious or worried a lot?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve probably always been someone who thinks things through too much, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I would’ve called it “worried a lot” before.\n\nLike, in school I was organized and I’d get stressed about assignments, but a lot of people do that. And at work, before all this, I could usually just handle things and move on. Sorry, I’m kind of comparing every version of myself now. I guess… I don’t really know where the normal stress line was before the condo stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having sudden episodes of intense panic or fear that peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it “panic,” exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel really scared or overwhelmed, but the “peaks within minutes” part—I’m not sure.\n\nLike, sometimes my heart jumps if I see an email from the mortgage company, and then I’ll start thinking about ten other things, like work and money and whether I should’ve handled something differently, and then suddenly I’m checking my phone again… Sorry, I’m kind of wandering.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed these sudden, out-of-nowhere episodes that hit a peak really fast. Yeah… I think so. I mean, it starts with something real, like the condo, so part of me says, “Of course you’re worried.” But then it doesn’t stay there.

It just keeps looping back.\n\nAnd then one little “what if” turns into, like, five more—like what if I missed something, what if someone notices, what if it causes a bigger problem… I mean, it sounds kind of silly when I say it out loud, because it’s usually not that dramatic. But in the moment, it takes a while before I can pull myself back from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different areas of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It doesn’t stay in one lane. Like, at first I thought it was just about the condo stuff—because there’s always something with the condo, right? Maintenance, neighbors, fees, whatever—but then it’s like my brain just… jumps.\n\nI’ll start worrying that my cousin is upset with me because she texted a little differently, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I’m going to mess up on a support call at work, and then it turns into this whole thing about money—like how am I ever going to save enough or move forward in my life. It’s not really one topic. It sort of bounces around.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after something particularly stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it really started around when the condo loan started heading toward foreclosure. That was, um, about nine months ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because of that specific mess—like all the paperwork and calls, and I kept thinking about the mail piling up even when it was just normal junk mail—but then it kind of stayed with me. So… yes, there was definitely that stressful thing, and the feelings have been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get upset and kind of worn out, but “depressed” feels like a big word? I guess I have days where I’m just not in a great mood, especially if something annoying happens—like yesterday my printer decided to stop connecting to Wi-Fi and I spent way too long messing with it, which is ridiculous because I troubleshoot stuff for work.\n\nBut, yeah… I’m not sure I’d say I feel down a lot of the time. I haven’t really been tracking it like that.\n\nDoctor: For the past six months or more, have you felt worried or on edge most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think so. It’s been about nine months now, since the whole condo loan thing started moving toward foreclosure. At first I kept telling myself, like, anyone would be stressed about that, which is probably true, but it didn’t really let up. It’s just sort of been there in the background most days since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Most nights I’m exhausted by the time I get into bed, but then my brain just… turns on. I’ll start thinking about the loan notices, or some work ticket I maybe didn’t handle right, or something I forgot to check—like it becomes this whole mental checklist.\n\nAnd then suddenly it’s 2 or 3 in the morning, and I’m still awake. Even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I barely rested. It’s frustrating, because I’ll be so tired, but I just can’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry is stronger than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the condo thing is a real problem, so part of me keeps saying, of course I’m worried. But it doesn’t stay there. It jumps around to everything—like whether my cousin is mad at me because she replied with just “okay,” or whether I’m going to mess up a support call at work, or how I’m ever supposed to save money and actually move forward.\n\nAnd then I’ll be doing something totally normal, like making coffee, and suddenly I’m thinking through five different things that could go wrong. So… yeah, I guess it probably gets bigger than the situation sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I feel kind of wound up almost all the time, like my body is bracing for the next bad thing even when I’m just sitting at my desk. My shoulders especially get tight, and sometimes I don’t even notice until I’m like, why am I sitting like this?\n\nIt’s weird because even at dance night, which is supposed to be fun, I’ll catch myself kind of holding tension instead of just relaxing into it. Then I start thinking about whether I look awkward, which—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, I notice the tightness a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like most of your distress is mainly tied to the condo foreclosure situation?\n\nPatient: I mean, it started with the condo, and that’s still the big thing, obviously. But no… it doesn’t really stay there anymore.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to focus on something else and suddenly I’m worried my cousin is upset with me because of how she texted back, or I’m replaying a support call in my head and thinking I’m going to make some mistake, or I’m spiraling about how I’m ever supposed to save money and move forward. It kind of spreads into everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is busy with worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. Like at work I’ll be writing a script or helping someone on a support call—in English or Spanish—and then suddenly I’m just… staring at the screen. I’ll realize I’ve drifted into thinking about everything that could go wrong, and I completely lose the thread of what I was doing.\n\nSometimes I have to reread the same thing a few times, which is frustrating because normally I’m pretty good at that stuff. And then I start worrying that taking too long looks bad, which kind of makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: If the condo foreclosure were fully resolved tomorrow, do you think the anxiety would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I don’t think so. I wish I could say yes, because the condo thing is huge, but the worry doesn’t really stay there anymore.\n\nEven if that disappeared tomorrow, I think my brain would just grab onto something else—like whether my cousin is mad at me because her text sounded short, or whether I’m going to mess up on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and actually move forward. It’s like it jumps topics.\n\nI mean, resolving the condo would help, definitely. But I don’t think it would make the anxiety mostly go away.\n\nDoctor: Has this anxiety been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I keep wanting to say I’m managing, because technically I still show up to work, but I’ve started falling behind on support tickets. And sometimes I’ll double-check really simple things over and over, like something I know how to do, and then I lose time.\n\nA couple coworkers have had to pick up the slack, which I feel awful about. And outside of work, I’ve skipped dance nights and mentoring sessions because I just feel too overwhelmed to go. Which is frustrating, because those are things I actually care about, and normally they help me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Before the condo foreclosure started, were you already someone who felt anxious or worried a lot?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve probably always been someone who thinks things through too much, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I would’ve called it “worried a lot” before.\n\nLike, in school I was organized and I’d get stressed about assignments, but a lot of people do that. And at work, before all this, I could usually just handle things and move on. Sorry, I’m kind of comparing every version of myself now. I guess… I don’t really know where the normal stress line was before the condo stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having sudden episodes of intense panic or fear that peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it “panic,” exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel really scared or overwhelmed, but the “peaks within minutes” part—I’m not sure.\n\nLike, sometimes my heart jumps if I see an email from the mortgage company, and then I’ll start thinking about ten other things, like work and money and whether I should’ve handled something differently, and then suddenly I’m checking my phone again… Sorry, I’m kind of wandering.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed these sudden, out-of-nowhere episodes that hit a peak really fast. It feels more like the anxiety builds or just sort of stays there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I try to, like—I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You already sent that email, it’s fine,” but then my brain keeps circling back to it.\n\nAnd then it’s not even just that one thing anymore. It’ll turn into, “What if I missed something?” and then “What if someone notices?” and then five other what-ifs, and suddenly I’m just… stuck in it. Sometimes I can pull myself back, but it takes a while.\n\nDoctor: When you worry, does it usually feel out of proportion to what’s actually happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it starts with something real, like the condo, so part of me says, “Of course you’re worried.” But then it doesn’t stay there.\n\nI’ll be thinking about that and then suddenly I’m wondering if my cousin is upset with me because she answered a text kind of short, or whether I’m going to mess up on a support call at work, or how I’m ever supposed to save money and move forward with my life. And then I’m like… wait, how did I get from one email to my entire future falling apart?\n\nSo, yeah, I guess a lot of the time it feels bigger than what’s actually happening right then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
